<a id="tree:section"></a>
### Conditioned trees and uniform path sums

We isolate the probabilistic statement needed to pass from the block tree to graph distances. Conditioned-tree height limits originate in <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Aldous1993](#ref-Aldous1993)]; their stable-domain extension is developed in <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Duquesne2003](#ref-Duquesne2003)]. Joint depth-first process convergence to the same excursion was proved by Marckert and Mokkadem under stronger moment conditions <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[MarckertMokkadem2003](#ref-MarckertMokkadem2003)]. We invoke the finite-variance, attainable-size formulation of Broutin and Marckert <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[BroutinMarckert2014](#ref-BroutinMarckert2014)] for the tree geometry. We then prove a uniform law of large numbers for marked ancestral paths, using only a finite second offspring moment.

<a id="tree:assumptions"></a>
Let $\xi$ have distribution $(p_k)_{k\geq0}$ supported on the even nonnegative integers, with $$p_0p_2>0,\qquad \mathbb E\xi=1,\qquad
 0<\sigma^2:=\operatorname{Var}(\xi)<\infty.
 \label{tree:assumptions}$$ Write $\mathcal T$ for the corresponding plane Galton–Watson tree and $\mathcal T_N$ for its law conditional on having $N$ nodes. Its attainable sizes are exactly the odd integers: every tree satisfies $N-1=\sum_v\deg^+(v)$, and every odd size can be realized using only offspring numbers zero and two. All limits in this section run through these odd integers. Given the offspring numbers, attach independent marks $A_v$ to the nodes, with a fixed law $\mathcal L_k$ at a node with $k$ children.

Index the nodes in preorder by $v_0,\ldots,v_{N-1}$. Let $H(v)$ denote depth, put $H_j=H(v_j)$ for $j<N$ and $H_N=0$, and define the exploration walk by <a id="tree:walk-definition"></a>$$W_0=0,\qquad W_{j+1}-W_j=\deg^+(v_j)-1
 \quad(0\leq j<N).
 \label{tree:walk-definition}$$ Thus $W_j\geq0$ for $j<N$ and $W_N=-1$. Interpolate both sequences linearly between integer times, and write $W(v_j)=W_j$.

<a id="stmt-tree-excursion"></a>
<div id="tree:excursion" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16** (Joint coding limit). *Under <a href="#tree:assumptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tree:assumptions">[tree:assumptions]</a>, <a id="tree:walk-limit"></a>$$\left(\frac{W_{Nt}}{\sigma\sqrt N},
       \frac{\sigma H_{Nt}}{2\sqrt N}\right)_{0\leq t\leq1}
 \ \Longrightarrow\ (e(t),e(t))_{0\leq t\leq1}
 \quad\hbox{in }C([0,1],\mathbb R^2),
 \label{tree:walk-limit}$$ where $e$ is a standard normalized Brownian excursion.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tree-excursion"><summary>Proof of stmt-tree-excursion</summary>

*Proof.* Broutin and Marckert’s Theorem 3  gives the joint height and walk limit for uniform plane trees whose degree frequencies and second moments converge to a critical finite-variance law, with maximum degree $o(\sqrt N)$. Their Lemma 11 verifies these hypotheses in probability for conditioned critical Galton–Watson trees; their Section 6 explicitly takes all attainable sizes.

Given the offspring counts $(n_k)$, our tree is uniform, since each shape has probability $\prod_kp_k^{n_k}$. To apply the deterministic theorem to these random counts, take any subsequence and then a further subsequence on which its degree hypotheses hold almost surely. Conditional expectations of bounded continuous tests converge by the theorem; dominated convergence removes the conditioning. This proves the limit along the full admissible sequence.

The source parametrizes height by $(N-1)t$. Continuous-path tightness makes the change to $Nt$ negligible before the last interval. On that interval, $H_{N-1}/\sqrt N\to0$ in probability because the limiting excursion ends at zero, so appending $H_N=0$ is also harmless. The marks do not affect the tree-shape law. ◻

</details>

Put $\nu=\sigma^2/2$ and let $\Delta_N$ be the largest offspring number.

<a id="stmt-tree-maxdegree"></a>
<div id="tree:maxdegree" class="corollary">

<a id="tree:walk-height-estimate"></a>
**Corollary 17**. *We have <a id="tree:degree-bound"></a>$$\max_v H(v)=O_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt N),\qquad
 \max_v W(v)=O_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt N),\qquad
 \Delta_N=o_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt N),
 \label{tree:degree-bound}$$ and $$\max_v|W(v)-\nu H(v)|=o_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt N).
 \label{tree:walk-height-estimate}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tree-maxdegree"><summary>Proof of stmt-tree-maxdegree</summary>

*Proof.* The height and walk bounds follow from Proposition <a href="#tree:excursion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tree:excursion">16</a>. Since both normalized processes converge jointly to the same excursion, their uniform difference tends to zero in probability, proving <a href="#tree:walk-height-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tree:walk-height-estimate">[tree:walk-height-estimate]</a>. Continuous-path tightness also makes the largest walk increment divided by $\sqrt N$ tend to zero. An offspring number is a walk increment plus one. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-tree-conditioning"></a>
<div id="tree:conditioning" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (Cost of conditioning). *As $N$ tends to infinity through odd integers, <a id="tree:size-probability"></a>$$\pi_N:=\mathbb P(|\mathcal T|=N)
 \sim\frac{2}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}N^{-3/2}.
 \label{tree:size-probability}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tree-conditioning"><summary>Proof of stmt-tree-conditioning</summary>

*Proof.* Let $X_1,X_2,\ldots$ be independent with law $\xi-1$, and put $S_r=\sum_{j=1}^rX_j$. The exploration fills one waiting slot and creates $\xi$ new ones at each step. Thus total size $N$ means that $S$ first reaches $-1$ at $N$. A list of increments at least $-1$ totaling $-1$ has exactly one circular shift with this property: extend the list periodically, decreasing its height by one per period, and observe that its strict descending record endpoints occur once per period. Cyclic symmetry therefore gives the Otter–Dwass formula (see also <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Janson2012](#ref-Janson2012), Theorem 15.5]) $$\pi_N=\frac1N\mathbb P(S_N=-1).$$ The lattice span of $X$ is exactly two because $-1$ and $1$ both have positive probability. At compatible sites $j\equiv r\pmod2$, the finite-variance local limit estimate is $$\mathbb P(S_r=j)=\frac{2}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi r}}
             \exp\left(-\frac{j^2}{2r\sigma^2}\right)
             +o(r^{-1/2}),$$ uniformly in $j$. One can obtain this directly by Fourier inversion on $[-\pi/2,\pi/2]$ with prefactor $1/\pi$: the characteristic function is $1-\sigma^2\theta^2/2+o(\theta^2)$ near zero and has modulus strictly less than one elsewhere on that interval. Rescaling $\theta$ by $r^{-1/2}$ gives the Gaussian integral, with uniformly vanishing integrated error. Taking $r=N$ and $j=-1$ proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### A spine law and bounded labels

<a id="tree:waiting-slots"></a>
<a id="tree:mirror-estimate"></a>
For each strict ancestor of a node $v$, let $k$ be the ancestor’s number of children and $i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}$ the child leading toward $v$. The waiting slots in the preorder exploration give $$W(v)=\sum_{\operatorname{path}(v)}(k-i),
 \qquad
 \widehat W(v):=\sum_{\operatorname{path}(v)}(i-1).
 \label{tree:waiting-slots}$$ The second quantity is the preorder walk value at the corresponding node after reversing every child order. Reflection preserves the law of $\mathcal T_N$ and preserves depths. Hence Corollary <a href="#tree:maxdegree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tree:maxdegree">17</a> also gives $$\max_v|\widehat W(v)-\nu H(v)|=o_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt N).
 \label{tree:mirror-estimate}$$ Only the tree shape is used here; no symmetry of the marks is required.

<a id="tree:spine-law"></a>
<a id="tree:spine-identity"></a>
The size-biased spine construction is classical; see Lyons, Pemantle, and Peres <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[LyonsPemantlePeres1995](#ref-LyonsPemantlePeres1995), Section 2]. For the marks and chosen child used here, define a probability law on triples $(K,I,A)$ by $$\mathbb P_{\mathrm{sp}}(K=k,I=i,A\in B)=p_k\mathcal L_k(B),
 \qquad 1\leq i\leq k.
 \label{tree:spine-law}$$ The total mass is $\mathbb E\xi=1$, and <a id="tree:spine-means"></a>$$\mathbb E_{\mathrm{sp}}(K-I)=\frac{\mathbb E[\xi(\xi-1)]}{2}=\nu,
 \qquad \mathbb E_{\mathrm{sp}}K=2\nu+1<\infty.
 \label{tree:spine-means}$$ For independent triples $Z_1,\ldots,Z_h$ with this law and every nonnegative path function $F$, independence in the unconditioned tree gives the path-counting identity $$\mathbb E\left[\sum_{H(v)=h}F(\operatorname{path}(v))\right]
 =\mathbb E_{\mathrm{sp}}F(Z_1,\ldots,Z_h).
 \label{tree:spine-identity}$$ Indeed, at each ancestor one sums over its possible child choices; the probability of each pair $(k,i)$, including its independent mark, is exactly the factor in <a href="#tree:spine-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tree:spine-law">[tree:spine-law]</a>.

For a label $b=b(k,i,A)$, set $$B_b(v)=\sum_{\operatorname{path}(v)}b(k,i,A),\qquad
 \mu_b=\mathbb E_{\mathrm{sp}}b(K,I,A)$$ whenever the expectation exists.

<a id="stmt-tree-bounded-paths"></a>
<div id="tree:bounded-paths" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (Bounded path sums). *For every fixed bounded measurable real label $b$, <a id="tree:bounded-sums"></a>$$\max_{v\in\mathcal T_N}|B_b(v)-\mu_bH(v)|
 =o_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt N).
 \label{tree:bounded-sums}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tree-bounded-paths"><summary>Proof of stmt-tree-bounded-paths</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $C,\varepsilon>0$. The exponential estimate for bounded independent variables gives, uniformly for $h\leq C\sqrt N$, $$\mathbb P_{\mathrm{sp}}\left(
 \left|\sum_{r=1}^h b(Z_r)-\mu_bh\right|>\varepsilon\sqrt N
 \right)\leq C' e^{-c'\sqrt N}.$$ For short paths the event may be empty; otherwise the usual bound $2\exp(-c\varepsilon^2N/h)$ gives the displayed estimate. The probability under $\mathcal T_N$ that any such path violates the desired bound is at most $$\pi_N^{-1}\sum_{h\leq C\sqrt N}C'e^{-c'\sqrt N}=o(1),$$ by the unconditioned identity <a href="#tree:spine-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tree:spine-identity">[tree:spine-identity]</a> and Lemma <a href="#tree:conditioning" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tree:conditioning">18</a>. Now let $C$ increase, using maximum-depth tightness from Corollary <a href="#tree:maxdegree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tree:maxdegree">17</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Labels bounded by the offspring number

We now allow labels that grow with the offspring number. The two exploration orders control the sum of offspring numbers along every ancestral path. Subtracting its bounded truncation will control the remaining tail uniformly.

<a id="stmt-tree-paths"></a>
<div id="tree:paths" class="theorem">

**Theorem 20** (Uniform marked path sums). *Let $b=b(k,i,A)$ be a fixed measurable label with $0\leq b\leq k$. Then $\mu_b<\infty$ and <a id="tree:marked-sums"></a>$$\max_{v\in\mathcal T_N}|B_b(v)-\mu_bH(v)|=o_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt N),
 \qquad
 \max_{v\in\mathcal T_N}
 \left|B_b(v)-\frac{\mu_b}{\nu}W(v)\right|=o_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt N).
 \label{tree:marked-sums}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tree-paths"><summary>Proof of stmt-tree-paths</summary>

<a id="tree:uniform-tail"></a>
*Proof.* The exact identities <a href="#tree:waiting-slots" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tree:waiting-slots">[tree:waiting-slots]</a> give <a id="tree:total-label"></a>$$\sum_{\operatorname{path}(v)}k=W(v)+\widehat W(v)+H(v).
 \label{tree:total-label}$$ By <a href="#tree:walk-height-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tree:walk-height-estimate">[tree:walk-height-estimate]</a> and <a href="#tree:mirror-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tree:mirror-estimate">[tree:mirror-estimate]</a>, this is $(2\nu+1)H(v)+o_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt N)$ uniformly in $v$. For each fixed $R$, Lemma <a href="#tree:bounded-paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tree:bounded-paths">19</a> applies to the bounded label $k\mathbf 1_{\{k\leq R\}}$. Subtracting its estimate yields <a id="tree:tail-estimate"></a>$$\sum_{\operatorname{path}(v)}k\mathbf 1_{\{k>R\}}
 =m_RH(v)+o_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt N)
 \quad\hbox{uniformly in }v,
 \qquad m_R:=\mathbb E_{\mathrm{sp}}[K\mathbf 1_{\{K>R\}}].
 \label{tree:tail-estimate}$$ The error statement here is for each fixed $R$. Integrability of $K$ gives $m_R\to0$, and maximum-depth tightness consequently implies $$\lim_{R\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}
 \mathbb P\left(\max_v\sum_{\operatorname{path}(v)}
 k\mathbf 1_{\{k>R\}}>\varepsilon\sqrt N\right)=0
 \quad(\varepsilon>0).
 \label{tree:uniform-tail}$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#tree:bounded-paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tree:bounded-paths">19</a> to $b\mathbf 1_{\{k\leq R\}}$. The discarded path sum is bounded by the tail in <a href="#tree:uniform-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tree:uniform-tail">[tree:uniform-tail]</a>, and the discarded spine mean is bounded by $m_R$. Letting first $N$ and then $R$ tend to infinity proves the first assertion. The second follows from <a href="#tree:walk-height-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tree:walk-height-estimate">[tree:walk-height-estimate]</a> and $\nu>0$. ◻

</details>

This truncation uses $\mathbb E_{\mathrm{sp}}K=\mathbb E\xi^2<\infty$; it does not require a second moment of the size-biased variable $K$, and hence does not impose a third offspring moment.

We finish with a deterministic comparison for later use.

<a id="stmt-tree-lca"></a>
<div id="tree:lca" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21** (Common ancestors and walk minima). *If $j\leq j'$ and $v_*$ is the lowest common ancestor of $v_j,v_{j'}$, then <a id="tree:lca-walk"></a>$$W(v_*)\leq\min_{j\leq r\leq j'}W_r\leq W(v_*)+\Delta_N.
 \label{tree:lca-walk}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tree-lca"><summary>Proof of stmt-tree-lca</summary>

*Proof.* Every intervening node is a descendant of $v_*$. Its walk value is at least $W(v_*)$ by <a href="#tree:waiting-slots" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tree:waiting-slots">[tree:waiting-slots]</a>. If $v_*=v_j$, the upper bound is immediate. Otherwise the interval contains the child of $v_*$ leading toward $v_{j'}$; its walk value exceeds $W(v_*)$ by at most the offspring number of $v_*$. ◻

</details>

<a id="metric:section"></a>
### From the block tree to the map

The mean block-distance comparison follows the general strategy for subcritical graph classes <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[PanagiotouStuflerWeller2016](#ref-PanagiotouStuflerWeller2016), Section 5] and tree-like maps <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Stufler2020](#ref-Stufler2020), preprint Section 6.8]. Here Theorem <a href="#tree:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tree:paths">20</a> supplies the required uniformity under only finite offspring variance. We also retain the exact corner-mass calculation, as in <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[Stufler2026](#ref-Stufler2026), Section 9], because the conclusion specifies the degree probability measure.

<a id="metric:beta"></a>
Let $\mathsf T_N$ be the conditioned marked tree of Proposition <a href="#block:finite-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="block:finite-law">15</a>, where $N=2n+1$. Its map has the required marginal law of $M_n$. Write $\pi(w)$ for the pole of a tree node $w$. For a strict ancestor with $k$ children, block mark $B$, and selected child $i$, put $$g(k,i,B)=d_B\big(\text{pole of }B,\text{vertex of corner }i\big),
 \qquad \beta=\mathbb E_{\rm sp}g.$$ The expectation is under the spine law <a href="#tree:spine-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tree:spine-law">[tree:spine-law]</a>, and $d_B$ uses all block edges. Since a block has $k/2$ edges, $0\le g\le k$. Therefore $$\label{metric:beta}
 0<\beta<\infty.$$ Finiteness follows from $\mathbb E_{\rm sp}k=\mathbb E\xi^2<\infty$. For positivity, a one-link block and its corner at the endpoint opposite the pole have positive spine probability, and their distance is one.

<a id="metric:uniform-poles"></a>
Let $G(w)$ be the sum of these $g$ labels over the strict ancestors of $w$. By Theorem <a href="#tree:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tree:paths">20</a> and the coding estimates in Section <a href="#tree:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tree:section">5</a>, with $\nu=\sigma^2/2$, $$\label{metric:uniform-poles}
 \max_{w\in\mathsf T_N}
 \left|G(w)-\beta H(w)\right|=o_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt N),\qquad
 \max_{w\in\mathsf T_N}
 \left|G(w)-\frac{\beta}{\nu}W(w)\right|=o_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt N).$$

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Uniform comparison of distances

<a id="stmt-metric-poles"></a>
<div id="metric:poles" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22** (Distances between poles). *Let $w_*$ be the last common ancestor of two tree nodes $w,w'$. If $\Delta$ is the maximum offspring number, then $$\left|d_{M_n}\big(\pi(w),\pi(w')\big)
       -\big(G(w)+G(w')-2G(w_*)\big)\right|\le2\Delta.$$ The map $\pi$ from tree nodes to map vertices is onto.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-metric-poles"><summary>Proof of stmt-metric-poles</summary>

*Proof.* Every inserted map meets the preceding part at its pole only. An excursion from a block into an attached submap must return to the same vertex, so attachments cannot shorten distances within a block. Moving from an ancestor pole to a descendant pole therefore adds the distances $g$ along their chain of blocks.

If one of $w,w'$ is their last common ancestor, this proves the displayed comparison with zero error. Otherwise let $x,x'$ be the two selected corner vertices in the block of $w_*$. The route through the pole of that block has length $d_B(x,\pi(w_*))+d_B(\pi(w_*),x')$ within the block, whereas the shortest route uses $d_B(x,x')$. Their difference lies between zero and twice the diameter of $B$, and that diameter is at most its number of edges, hence at most $\Delta$. Outside the common block the distances add exactly as above. The same argument allows coincident poles and loop blocks.

Every vertex of the nonempty map is incident to at least one edge, and hence to a corner in one of its blocks. That corner is a child slot, whose child’s pole is the given vertex. Thus every map vertex is the pole of a nonroot node. ◻

</details>

<a id="metric:walk-comparison"></a>
Index the nodes by $j=0,\ldots,N-1$ in preorder. Combining Lemma <a href="#metric:poles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:poles">22</a>, <a href="#metric:uniform-poles" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="metric:uniform-poles">[metric:uniform-poles]</a>, and Lemma <a href="#tree:lca" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tree:lca">21</a>, and using $\Delta=o_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt N)$, gives $$\label{metric:walk-comparison}
 \max_{0\le j\le j'<N}
 \left|d_{M_n}\big(\pi(j),\pi(j')\big)
  -\frac{\beta}{\nu}
       \left(W_j+W_{j'}-2\min_{j\le r\le j'}W_r\right)\right|
   =o_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt N).$$ In particular, the comparison is uniform over every map vertex, including vertices in the largest blocks.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### The degree measure

<a id="stmt-metric-measure"></a>
<div id="metric:measure" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (Corner mass). *The uniform probability measure on the $N-1=2n$ nonroot tree nodes, pushed forward by $\pi$, is exactly $\mu_n$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-metric-measure"><summary>Proof of stmt-metric-measure</summary>

*Proof.* Each nonroot node occupies one corner of its parent block, and every block corner is occupied once, including the corners whose inserts are trivial leaves. At a map vertex $v$, summing these corner counts over the blocks gives $\deg_{M_n}(v)$: each incident dart belongs to one block, and each block has one corner per incident dart. A loop contributes two darts and two corners at its vertex. Division by the total $2n$ proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="metric:mass-error"></a>
If $\bar\mu_n$ is instead the pushforward of uniform probability on all $N$ nodes, then $$\label{metric:mass-error}
 \bar\mu_n=\frac{N-1}{N}\mu_n+\frac1N\delta_{\pi(0)},
 \qquad
 \|\bar\mu_n-\mu_n\|_{\rm TV}\le\frac1N.$$ The measure identity is thus independent of the sizes and shapes of the individual blocks.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### A correspondence estimate

For completeness we state the elementary estimate used to pass to metric probability spaces. A *correspondence* between $X$ and $Y$ is a relation $\mathcal R\subseteq X\times Y$ projecting onto both spaces. Its distortion is $$\operatorname{dis}(\mathcal R)
 =\sup_{\substack{(x,y),(x',y')\in\mathcal R}}
       |d_X(x,x')-d_Y(y,y')|.$$ The Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov distance is the infimum, over common isometric embeddings, of the maximum of the Hausdorff distance between the embedded spaces and the Prokhorov distance between their probability measures.

<a id="stmt-metric-ghp"></a>
<div id="metric:ghp" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Metric and mass comparison). *Suppose a correspondence between compact metric probability spaces has distortion at most $\varepsilon>0$, and a coupling of their probability measures is supported on the correspondence. Their Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov distance is at most $\varepsilon$. If one marginal of that coupling differs from the desired measure by total variation at most $\eta$, the bound is $\varepsilon+\eta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-metric-ghp"><summary>Proof of stmt-metric-ghp</summary>

*Proof.* Join corresponding points by links of length $\varepsilon$, and use the induced shortest-chain distance on the disjoint union. This preserves the distances within each original space. Indeed, a segment leaving one space and returning to it can be replaced by a segment inside that space: the two linking lengths are $2\varepsilon$, whereas the difference of the distances between the corresponding endpoints is at most $\varepsilon$. Every point is within $\varepsilon$ of the other space, giving the Hausdorff bound. The coupling places every pair at distance at most $\varepsilon$, which gives the Prokhorov bound directly from its defining neighborhood inequalities. Changing one marginal by total variation $\eta$ increases these inequalities, and hence the Prokhorov bound, by at most $\eta$. ◻

</details>

For a nonnegative continuous function $f$ on $[0,1]$ with $f(0)=f(1)=0$, define $d_f$ by the same formula as $d_e$, and denote its quotient metric probability space by $\mathcal T_f$. The interval-minimum formula gives the triangle inequality for $d_f$. The quotient projection is continuous, since $d_f(s,t)$ is at most twice the oscillation of $f$ on the interval between $s$ and $t$. Thus $\mathcal T_f$ is compact. Relating equal-time representatives in $\mathcal T_f$ and $\mathcal T_{\tilde f}$ gives a correspondence of distortion at most $4\|f-\tilde f\|_\infty$ and a coupling from one uniform time. Lemma <a href="#metric:ghp" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:ghp">24</a> therefore proves continuity of $$f\longmapsto \mathcal T_f$$ from the uniform norm to the Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov topology.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Proof of the main theorem

<details id="proof-stmt-metric-ghp-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-metric-ghp</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Define <a id="metric:constant"></a>$$\label{metric:constant}
 c(q)=\frac{\nu}{\sqrt2\,\beta\sigma}
     =\frac{\sigma}{2\sqrt2\,\beta}.$$ Equations <a href="#block:moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="block:moments">[block:moments]</a> and <a href="#metric:beta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="metric:beta">[metric:beta]</a> show that this constant is deterministic, positive, and finite for every fixed $q>4$.

Let $f_N$ interpolate $W_j/(\sigma\sqrt N)$ at $j/N$ for $0\le j<N$, and set $f_N(1)=0$. It is nonnegative and has zero endpoints. Replacing the exploration walk’s last value $W_N=-1$ by zero changes its normalized interpolation by at most $1/(\sigma\sqrt N)$. Proposition <a href="#tree:excursion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tree:excursion">16</a> consequently gives $$f_N\Longrightarrow e
 \qquad\text{in }C([0,1]).$$ For grid points $j/N,j'/N$, the comparison <a href="#metric:walk-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="metric:walk-comparison">[metric:walk-comparison]</a> and the identity $N=2n+1$ imply $$\max_{j,j'<N}
 \left|
  \frac{c(q)}{\sqrt n}\,
     d_{M_n}\big(\pi(j),\pi(j')\big)
  -\sqrt{\frac{N}{2n}}\,
     d_{f_N}(j/N,j'/N)
 \right|=o_{\mathbb P}(1).$$ Since $\sqrt{N/(2n)}\to1$ and $\|f_N\|_\infty$ is tight, that deterministic factor may be replaced by one.

For $s\in[0,1]$, put $j_N(s)=\min\{\lfloor Ns\rfloor,N-1\}$ and write $[s]$ for its class in $\mathcal T_{f_N}$. The relation $$\mathcal R_N=\{(\pi(j_N(s)),[s]):s\in[0,1]\}$$ is a correspondence by Lemma <a href="#metric:poles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:poles">22</a>. Rounding times changes a $d_{f_N}$ distance by at most $4\omega_{f_N}(1/N)$, where $\omega$ is the uniform modulus of continuity. Continuous-path tightness makes this error tend to zero in probability. The correspondence therefore has distortion $o_{\mathbb P}(1)$ for the rescaled map metric.

A Lebesgue-uniform time couples the probability measure of $\mathcal T_{f_N}$ to $\bar\mu_n$ exactly, since each of the $N$ preorder cells has length $1/N$. By Lemma <a href="#metric:ghp" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="metric:ghp">24</a> and <a href="#metric:mass-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="metric:mass-error">[metric:mass-error]</a>, the Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov distance from the rescaled map with measure $\mu_n$ to $\mathcal T_{f_N}$ tends to zero in probability. The continuity established above and $f_N\Longrightarrow e$ prove the claimed convergence to $(\mathcal T_e,d_e,\mu_e)$.

Proposition <a href="#block:finite-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="block:finite-law">15</a> applies to every $n\ge1$, and the conditioned-tree results hold through all the corresponding odd sizes. The proof uses only the map marginal and all-edge block distances, so its conclusion is precisely the unrooted, undecorated metric probability limit stated in the theorem. ◻

</details>
